<meta>This page continues the 9th Question from the previous page, further exploring the relationship between God's Love and God's Wrath. It explains why an omnipotent God does not "force" the rebellious will to submit, focusing on the distinct but originating-from-the-same-source nature of these two divine principles.</meta>

Question 9. Contemplation of Divine Revelation.

they are indeed both called **God**, but **God**, insofar as He is the eternal Good, is not the Wrath; the Wrath has a different <term>Principle<note>original: "Principium." In Böhme's philosophy, a "principle" refers to a fundamental source or realm of reality, such as the Light-world of love or the Dark-world of fire.</note></term>. In the fire of Love they are certainly one, but in separation they are two. And because they are both eternal without beginning, they also possess an eternal will, wherein neither may kill the other, but each remains eternal within itself: it is only a twofold power, and they are two <term>Centers<note>original: "Centra." Internal focal points or sources of spiritual energy and manifestation.</note></term>, yet they originate from unity, from a single Ground.

4. Therefore, when I say of **God's** love: it is Almighty, above all and in all, this happens according to the will of the **Jah** <note>original: "Jah." A Hebrew name for God, which Böhme uses here to represent the divine "Yes," the eternal affirmation and Light.</note>, as the light; and if the "No" <note>The "No" represents the dark, contracting, and resisting principle.</note> gives its will to it, then the **Jah** transforms the "No" into its power and love, and yet two central wills remain within one another, but in one Ground, in one love and desire; otherwise, the wrathful **God** would not be almighty if love alone held him in her omnipotence—and though it is indeed only one **God**, love would not be manifest, and no love would be known without the wrath.

5. Therefore, love surrenders itself to the fire of wrath so that it may be a fire of love; however, if the wrath separates itself from love into its own self-satisfaction, love does not prevent this with force, otherwise it would follow that **God** would become divided within Himself.

6. Thus, the "No"—as **God's** wrath—has, in the fallen angels, entered into a separation from love as into its own kingdom. This is also a wonder, and even a good thing, so that the other angels might have a mirror, and so that their own self-will does not exalt itself; also, so that there may be an eternal joy and praise of **God**, that they do not live in such a fire-essence, but rather turn their desire all the more toward **God’s** unity, remaining in <term>yieldedness<note>original: "Gelassenheit." A key term in German mysticism meaning a state of humble, passive surrender to God’s will, letting go of the "self."</note></term> and humility, as in divine harmony.

7. When we speak here of the will of **God’s** wrath—that it broke itself off from love and desired to be figurative <note>Meaning to take a manifested, distinct form or "image" in the physical/spiritual world.</note>—this must not be understood as happening outside the creature. It was not a foreign will that was not also figurative within the fallen angels; rather, it was the wrathful **God** within the formed creature, which previously had been figurative. Otherwise, their entire <term>place<note>original: "Locus." The spiritual station or region the angels occupied.</note></term> would have been a falling away; but that it is not so is proven by the fact that they were cast out from their throne.

<meta>catchword: 8. One</meta>

<summary>Böhme explains that God's Love and Wrath are two eternal principles from the same source; the "No" of the Wrath allows Love to be manifested and known. He argues that the fall of angels serves as a "mirror" for others to remain in humble yieldedness.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the nature of divine judgment and the origin of evil, moving from the general concept of God’s "Wrath" into the specific fall of Lucifer (the Devil). It transitions from Question 9 into Question 10.</meta>

XVIII. Theosophical Questions. <note>original: "Quæstiones Theosophicæ." These are a series of philosophical and theological inquiries written by Böhme to explain the mysteries of the divine nature.</note> Fragment 10.

8. One must not blame **God** for the Fall, but rather only the formed power <note>original: "gebildete Kraft." This refers to the specific potential or energy that has taken shape within a created being, as opposed to the unformed, pure essence of God.</note> in the creature according to the "No"; this power has forfeited its state and become a lie—not **God**, but the creature. It was not the unformed power of the wrath, in which love burns, but rather the "throne" <note>This refers to the angelic hierarchy or the high station Lucifer held before his fall.</note> according to its own desire and selfhood. And as their king and prince did, so also did all his legions.

9. For when **God’s** wrath hardens a creature, it is not an external fury that enters into them to harden them, but rather their own fury that does so. Because of this, a judgment may be passed upon them. Otherwise, if **God** forced them toward evil, no judgment could be passed upon them, for they would only be doing what **God** wanted. But as it stands, the creature does what **God** does *not* want from them; therefore, they fall into judgment.

10. Reason <note>original: "Vernunft." In Böhme's writing, "Reason" often refers to the limited, earthly intellect that tries to understand divine things without spiritual illumination.</note> talks much about **God** and His omnipotence, but it understands little of **God** and His essence—what and how He is. It separates the soul entirely from **God**, as if it were merely a separate being, and does not know what an angel or a soul is. This is the great tragedy of blindness, which leads people to quarrel and dispute without ever reaching the true foundation.

->The 10th Question.<-

## What did the devil desire, that he strayed from God’s love?

->Summaries.<-

The devil understood the ground of creation and wanted to rule in all things as **God**, §. 1. but he did not know that his light was destined to go out. 2. He wanted to be lord in the "No," 3. and to work with the power of fire, 4. thinking the fire-nature was stronger than gentleness. 5. But the Holy Name of **Jesus** separated from him, and the darkness in him became manifest. 6. 7.

1. Answer. He desired to be an artist <note>original: "Künstler." In this context, it implies a master-craftsman or a magician who wants to manipulate the forces of creation according to his own will.</note>; he saw the creation and understood its foundation, and therein he wanted to be his own god. He wished to rule in all things with the central fire-power <note>original: "Centralischen Feuers-Macht." The raw, energetic source of life before it is softened by the Light.</note> and to form himself with all things. He also wanted to shape himself into all forms, so that he might be whatever he willed, and not what the Creator willed—just as it is still their greatest joy today that they can transform themselves.

<summary>This page explains that the Fall was caused by the creature's own self-centered will rather than God's intent, and begins exploring the Devil's specific desire to rule via fire-power and self-mastery.</summary>

# Fr. 10. Contemplation of Divine Revelation

...to transform, and to bring into various images, and thus to engage in <term>Fantasy</term> <note>original: "Phantasie." In Böhme’s writing, this refers to the deceptive or "false" power of imagination that creates mental phantoms rather than divine realities.</note>.

2. He wanted to be a fool, and that is exactly what he became; but he did not know what it would be like when the Light went out within him, just as Adam did not know what it would be like when he would feel heat and cold, and when the <term>Central Fire</term> <note>original: "Centralische Feuer." This refers to the core energetic source or "driving wheel" of a being's life, which can burn as either light or wrath.</note> with its properties would awaken within him, and that he would fall out of the state of balance and lose the Central Light-Fire; Adam did not know this either.

3. The Devil wanted to be a lord in the "No," <note>The "No" refers to the dark, wrathful, or negative principle of nature which stands in contrast to God’s "Yes" or Light.</note> and to possess a different <term>Principle</term> <note>original: "Principium." One of the fundamental realms of existence (Light, Dark, or the Material world).</note> than the power of distinction in the ground of figurative formation; he was not pleased by subjection, but rather by majesty, and he despised humility and <term>Gentleness</term> <note>original: "Süßigkeit (Sanftmuth)." This refers to the soft, loving quality of the Divine Light.</note> such as love and truth, and did not want to remain in the Unity, but rather in multiplicity.

4. He wanted to work with his own name, as if with the Power of the Central Fire, and not with the Name of **God** belonging to his throne; he set the "No" above the <term>Yes</term> <note>original: "Jah." Böhme uses "Jah" both as the Hebrew name for God and as a representation of the eternal "Yes" or positive affirmation of Divine Light.</note>. Thus he became an enemy of **God**, and a liar and a murderer of the good formation of the good powers; he desired to destroy what **God’s** working was forming, so that he might display his own working and formation.

5. The Magical Ground of the Omnipotence pleased him; for its sake he raised himself up and contradicted the "Yes," because in his own center it is not a working of fire; and because he saw that the "Yes"—as the outflow of the Unity—introduced itself into the Fire-Center as a Light and as active love, he thought that the Fire-nature was stronger and greater than the Gentleness of the Unity, and greater than the formed Names in the manifesting Unity, which he intended to lead within his Fire-power however he wished; and **God** had not created him for such a will.

6. Therefore, because he left **God's** order, the holy manifesting Name of **God** separated from him and remained in the Unity, while Lucifer remained in the properties of the Central Fire dwelling within himself. He still stood in his Princely Throne—in which the holy Name of **God** had formed itself into a throne—but he was no longer an angel with his legions, but rather a <term>mask-image</term> <note>original: "Larven-Bild." A hollow phantom or a deceptive appearance that lacks true divine life.</note> and a monster.

# XVIII. Theosophical Questions. Fragment 12.

->Summaries.<-

Love and Wrath stand in constant struggle. § 1. 2. The forces of nature also form themselves in a dark, hard manner. 3. Which formation of the Ideas has existed from eternity, yet not out of God’s formal deliberation, but only as a Play of Love. 4. The purpose stands in one's own will, 5. and is also the very foundation of the Devil; 6. against which formed false wills Michael fought. 7. Examples from Holy Scripture, Saul and Zedekiah. 8. In the Light is nothing but Love: 9. but in the power of the Fire arise the rays of Wrath, as "opinions," <note>original: "Meinungen." In Böhme's thought, "opinions" refer to fragmented, self-centered thoughts that deviate from the unified truth of God.</note> 10. which are nothing but evil spirits. 11. In this conflict, Jesus has immersed himself into humanity. 12. 14. Man is God’s image according to Love and Wrath, as 1) the soul, 2) the soul’s spirit, as the temple of the Holy Spirit, 15. 16. just as the body is from the elements and the stars. 17. To this spirit, along with the "Idea," Christ has come to help. 18. In which entry of Christ into the soul the Idea becomes movable and unites the "No" with the "Yes"; 19. thus in this light the soul also becomes seeing, and hears what God speaks within it. 20. In this way, man attains visual contemplation <note>original: "Schaulichkeit." This refers to a state of direct spiritual vision or intuitive understanding of divine realities.</note>. 21.

1. Answer. Dear reader, this question is placed here because unenlightened Reason <note>Reason (Vernunft) is often contrasted with spiritual Understanding (Verstand) in this period; Reason is seen as limited by the physical senses.</note> considers it impossible to know such mysteries, as it cannot grasp them; therefore, Reason slanders and attributes such things to the Devil. Thus, for the sake of love for our neighbor, we wish to explain it a little, so that many might become "seeing" and understand the deep meaning.

2. Reason runs in vain imagery according to the manner of creatures and thinks: God deliberated within His <term>Trinity: the Father, Son, and Holy Spirit</term> what He wanted to make and how things must and should go; because of this, Reason also wants to enclose the fall of Lucifer and Adam within a certain goal that God had ordained in His purpose. But the thinking of Reason is not the foundation; God does not need to deliberate about anything, for He Himself is the Counsel; the high, outflowing names of the powers are the Counsel of God, acting as the <term>Wonder-working Word: the creative Logos or speech of God that brings the universe into being</term>. There is also no pre-determined purpose for any thing, for the origin of all things—and how they can come to be—lies in the "Idea," in eternal formation. This is not a finished object, but exists in a state of constant formation where God’s Love and Wrath (as the two central fires of the powers) stand in a perpetual struggle of love. There, in such a wrestling of the powers, the Wonder-working Word forms itself into figures, which are nothing but counter-images of the Holy Names and powers, and these are called Ideas.

3. Conversely, the forces of eternal nature also form themselves

It critiques the idea that God "plans" like a human, arguing instead that the world is a continuous manifestation of the divine Word through "Ideas."</summary>

### Fr. 12. Contemplation of Divine Revelation.

...according to the nature of the formation toward fire, such as in a dark, sharp, hard, fierce, prickly, bitter, anxious, fiery, and cold way—which are not creatures <note>In Böhme’s thought, these are not individual beings yet, but the raw, energetic qualities of nature.</note>, but a formation of the powers in the <term>No</term> <note>original: "Nein." The "No" represents the negative, contractive, and self-centering principle of the universe.</note>, as in <term>God's Wrath</term>, and are yet always permeated with the <term>Yes</term> <note>original: "Jah." The "Yes" represents the affirmative, expansive principle of divine light and love.</note> and the Central Light, and transformed into a Play of Love, in the same way as a good thing comes from an evil one, or as a good holy angel comes from a damned soul or angel.

4. Such formation of the <term>Idea</term> <note>The eternal blueprint or spiritual prototype existing within God’s Wisdom.</note> has indeed been from eternity, as the Spirit of God has foreseen all things—what would and could be, if such a formation were brought into a creaturely nature: but it does not come from the divine counsel of the Holy Name, but rather originates in the flowed-out free will, according to both central fires of the powers, where the powers draw and form themselves; thus the self-will forms itself into the <term>No</term>, as into the fierce fire-nature; and the <term>Yes</term>, or the out-breathing of the Unity, forms itself into the light, and these two figures stand within one another in a single foundation. In the <term>Yes</term> stands the Holy Name (of Love) of GOD, and in the <term>No</term> stands the name of the Wrath of GOD: and there the <term>Yes</term> struggles in the Holy Name of Divine Love with the <term>No</term>, as with the flowed-out self-will of self-gratification, for the victory—and yet it is only a Play of Love. For the light permeates the formation of the darkness, and so the formation is set into a triumph of the kingdom of joy; and thus there is an eternal overcoming of the dragon in God’s wrath, and yet it is only a play of the two central fire-wills.

5. And here is the purpose of GOD, of which the Scripture speaks, and yet it stands in one’s own will: GOD, so far as He is and is called GOD, compels no figure, but the powers compel themselves for the sake of the victory.

6. And here is also the ground of the Devil: how the self-will, as the <term>No</term>, fought against the <term>Yes</term> in a formed manner, and departed from the <term>Yes</term>, and separated itself in its own movement, and became a devil, as an enemy against the <term>Yes</term>, and entered into its own figurative dominion.

7. Also, in this foundation, we see rightly how Michael fought against this dragon and the formed false wills,

### XVIII. Theosophical Questions. Fr. 12.

...contended <note>This continues the thought from the previous page regarding the internal struggle between the light and the fire.</note>, in which false will the <term>Yes</term> <note>original: "Jah." In Böhme's thought, the "Yes" is the divine principle of affirmation, light, and love.</note> nonetheless had been present initially as the holy name while they were still angels; but the self-will of the <term>Central Fire</term> <note>The "Central Fire" is the energetic core of a being. When aligned with God, it is a source of life; when separated, it becomes a consuming fire of ego and wrath.</note>'s own disharmony separated itself from the <term>Yes</term>, within itself, and for that reason, it was cast out from the <term>Yes</term> by the name of Michael <note>Referring to the Archangel Michael's defeat of Lucifer.</note>.

8. Such a thing is also to be proven in Holy Scripture, both with the prophet Micah and with King Saul, when an evil spirit from the Lord entered into Saul, and also into the prophets Zedekiah and his companions. Likewise, when the Lord spoke: "Who will entice Ahab the king, that he may go up to battle and fall at Ramoth in Gilead?" And one said this, and another said that, but a spirit went forth and stood before the Lord and said: "I will entice him." The Lord said to him: "With what?" He said: "I will go out and be a lying spirit in the mouth of all his prophets." He said: "You shall entice him and succeed; go out and do so." 1 Kings 22:20, 22. This "Lord" was the <term>Wrath of God</term> which burned over Israel, as can be proven in many places in the Scripture—especially in Moses and the prophets—where the Lord threatened them in his <term>Fire-Power</term>, saying He would devour them.

9. Thus we now rightly understand on such a foundation that such an outpouring of the Wrath of God comes from this basis; for in the holy name of God, as in the <term>Center of Light</term>, there is no wrathful will, but pure love, which is God Himself.

10. But in the <term>No</term> <note>original: "Nein." The "No" is the contractive, dark principle of nature that provides the necessary friction or "fire" for life to exist, but becomes "wrath" if it seeks to rule on its own.</note>, as in the Fire-Power, such rays of wrath arise; and these are the evil spirits in Saul, and in the false prophets, and also in the strife at <term>Babel</term> <note>Böhme uses "Babel" to represent the state of spiritual confusion, sectarianism, and the institutionalized church that relies on human intellect rather than divine spirit.</note> among the sects, regarding opinions in religion. Where one does not love God purely and cling to Him alone, but wishes only to run in "Art and Reason" <note>Human scholarly cleverness and intellectual logic divorced from spiritual experience.</note>, then such springs of error, such evil spirits of contention, rise up from the fiery soul—which has sprung from this foundation of the eternal Central Fire and has entered into a creature.

11. These same springs are purely evil spirits from the Lord of the Wrath of God, having sprung from the self-will of one's own disharmony, and are true heretics, as spewed-out

spirits, as they call themselves among one another; for one sees that they struggle only over the overcoming of mental images. For they struggle over the letter in the same manner as the internal conflict between the <term>Yes and No</term> <note>original: "Jah und Nein." In Böhme's thought, these are the two eternal principles: the Yes is the affirming light and love, while the No is the resisting fire and wrath.</note> happens between the two eternal principles.

15. Man is an image of the **Essence of all Essences**, a true likeness of God according to both Love and Wrath. First: (1) the soul is the eternal **central fire** of its own will; for creatures must come out of this fire, and not out of the pure, unmanifested God.

### XVIII. Theosophical Questions. Fr. 12.

...by which death and God’s wrath were broken: so then such Divine <term>Balsam</term> <note>original: "Balsam." In Böhme's writing, this refers to a life-giving, healing spiritual substance that counteracts the "poison" of wrath.</note> remains essential within the spirit of the soul, just as **Christ** says:

17. But (3) the outer body is from the outer world, from the four elements and the stars, which outer dominion rules the outer life. It was this outer dominion that the Adam-like soul lusted after, thereby breaking its will away from God’s Unity and introducing it into the rule of this world. There, the will was captured by the <term>dragon</term> <note>A symbol for the prideful, selfish, and wrathful nature of the fallen spirit.</note> of God’s wrath and transformed into a monster; there, the true spirit faded so that the Light of God was extinguished within it, and the *Idea* <note>original: "Idea." For Böhme, the *Idea* is the eternal, spiritual image or "template" of humanity that exists in God's wisdom; it became dormant or "mute" after the Fall.</note> became mute and ineffective.

18. To this spirit, along with the *Idea*, **JESUS** came as an outflow of Divine Unity to help, and brought the light of love back into the poor soul.

19. When this name **JESUS**—who is called **CHRIST** once He assumed humanity—moves into the poor, turned-away soul and penetrates it with this ray of God’s Unity, then the eternal *Idea*, as the true spirit, becomes active again within such a balsam of love. Thus, the light shines once more in the eternal darkness of the soul, and the "No" <note>The principle of wrath and resistance.</note> is again unified with the "Yes" <note>The principle of divine love and light.</note>; there **Michael** stands in battle against the dragon, as the name of the throne in **Christ JESUS**.

20. In this light, the soul looks back into its first homeland, its origin from which it sprouted; for the Name of God is within it and works in the spirit. There it stands in the <term>beholding of God</term> <note>original: "Schauen GOttes." This refers to mystical contemplation or the direct, non-intellectual vision of divine truth.</note> and may search all things—yet not through its own movement and will, but rather as the Name of God moves within it. Thus, the soul beholds the formation of the **Wonder-working Name**; it stands and hears what God speaks within it—not in figurative speech, but in actual understanding, just as the prophets spoke. Yet this is incomprehensible to outer reason, of which **St. Paul** says:
> Our citizenship is in heaven; (*Philippians* 3:20.)
and yet he also said that a thorn was given to him in the flesh, so that the messenger of Satan—as a torment of the <term>deluding spirit</term> <note>original: "Irrgeist." A spirit that leads the mind astray into worldly or selfish desires.</note> of lust—might buffet him with fists. (2 *Corinthians* 12:7).

21. So understand it rightly: A person sees such a mystery in the Spirit of Christ, in whom all treasures of wisdom lie, as Scripture testifies throughout. ## The 13th Question.

# How did the expulsion of the dragon and the legions of Lucifer occur; where was he cast, so that he can be outside of God, since God fills all things? Or what is the foundation of hell, in which he dwells?

### Summaries.

One's own false will must go out. §. 1. The dragon is the hellish foundation, 2. whose expulsion occurs through the power-name of the Throne, 3. as he dwells beneath the firmament, 4. and his dominion is in the elements, 5. in the out-flowed <term>wrath</term> <note>original: "Grimm." In Böhme's cosmology, "Grimm" or "Wrath" is the dark, harsh, and consuming quality of nature when it is not tempered by the light of God's love.</note>. 6. For within the elements, two kingdoms are understood, 7. where the good holds the evil captive; although the will of man often changes it. 8. The devil is a liar; 9. the dragon is a being of wrath, 10. and is separated from **God**: 11. his nature-fire is burning, 12. and compared to the heavenly, it is like night against day. 13. This abyss also pours itself out in many places, 14. although it is not yet entirely manifest. 15. **God** is **Nothing** in hell; 16. so also hell is **Nothing** in **God**. 17. In the light, **God** is <term>Something</term> <note>original: "Ichts." A term Böhme coined to represent "somethingness" or "substantiality," standing in contrast to the "Nichts" (Nothing).</note>: in hell, he is **Nothing**. 18. Therefore the devils stand in great mockery. 19.

1. **Answer.** The expulsion of the dragon was the false, turned-away, figurative will of self-gratification, in which turned-away will Lucifer and his angels formed themselves.

2. The dragon is the hellish foundation, and the cast-out spirits are the naturally formed qualities of the hellish foundation. For when they broke themselves off from the **Holy Name of God**, from the essence of **God’s** unity, and formed themselves entirely into the <term>No</term> <note>original: "Nein." This refers to the "Dark World" or the principle of negation and opposition to God's "Yes" (the light).</note>, as if into lies, they then became such as they are.

3. The expulsion occurred through the Holy Name of Power of the throne he once held; he was thrust from his throne with his legions, as if from his own dwelling, and was immediately enclosed by the darkness and seized by the <term>wrath</term> <note>original: "Grimm." In the author's system, this refers to the harsh, dark, and consuming quality of nature when it is separated from God’s light.</note> of the hellish foundation.

4. Thus, he is separated from **God’s** holiness and dwells beneath the firmament and upon the earth between time and eternity, as a Prince of Darkness within the wrath of **God**.

5. His princely rule is in the heights, but in various places—within the elements everywhere, according to the nature and property of the four elements and their characteristics. For their characteristics are manifold, and they have dwellings in all four elements, each tribe among them residing in what matches its own likeness.

6. However, this must be understood correctly: they do not possess the elements according to their good properties. No; they possess only the "out-flowed" wrath that stems from the eternal wrath of the hellish foundation.

7. For two kingdoms are always understood to exist within the elements: one according to **God’s** poured-out love, and the other according to his anger. So, the fallen spirits dwell only in the part of anger and are enclosed in eternal night. They cannot touch the good powers of the elements; however, whatever flows out of the wrath and forms itself into a physical being, they can certainly dwell within it. It is with this specific "wrathful" substance that sorcery is practiced.

8. There are indeed some powers in which the Holy Power of the central light-fire has also flowed out, which are entirely contrary to the hellish foundation. But in most (people), evil and good exist alongside one another, and the good holds the evil captive and permeates it so that the fallen spirits can achieve nothing within it—unless the human will changes it through its own power, or it is changed by the **Great Disturbance** <note>original: "Turbam Magnam." A Latin term Böhme uses to describe a state of chaos, judgment, or spiritual upheaval where the hidden wrath in nature breaks out and overcomes the good.</note>, such that a movement of wrath enters into a thing and the movement of evil surpasses the good.

9. Their dwelling is a pit of lies, a throat of eternal destruction, a formation of the <term>fantasy</term> <note>original: "Phantasie." For the author, this is not just idle dreaming, but a corrupted power of the mind to create false, non-divine realities.</note> with a false light. Through his <term>imagination</term> <note>original: "Imagination." This refers to the spiritual power to "image" or "form" one's internal state into a perceived reality.</note>, he fashions a light for himself out of this pit from the central fire, which

It also notes how human will or spiritual turmoil can allow this evil influence to overcome the natural good in the world.</summary>

# Fr. 13. Contemplation of Divine Revelation
### C 5

...stands in no foundation and does not touch **God’s** Majesty; it is extinguished if the **Name of God** within it is broken <note>In Böhme’s theology, the "Name of God" represents the holy, ordering principle of reality. To "break" the name is to separate a power from its divine source, which results in spiritual darkness.</note>, as is understood in the practice of sorcery.

10. This cast-out dragon—or the maw of the hellish foundation—is not itself the central fire of eternal nature, but rather a state of "un-pleasantness" <note>original: "Unnehmlichkeit." This implies a state of being that cannot be "taken in" or accepted by the light; a state of perpetual rejection.</note> of itself, like an outflow from fire. Just as smoke and fire are two distinct things and yet come from a single source, so too is he only a being of wrath, like the smoke that comes from the fire.

11. Therefore, he is entirely separated from **God**, as smoke is from fire; yet he receives power and strength from the central fire of eternal nature, for his life stands within it. But his will and desire resemble the smoke that rises from the fire.

12. For his natural fire in his creaturely (or central) essence is burning; but in **God’s** being, it is a <term>Temperamentum</term> <note>Latin: "A balance or proper mixing." In this context, it refers to the perfect harmony of qualities where the "fire" of existence is not painful, but is instead the warmth of love.</note> with the central love-fire of the light. The source of the fire is transformed into light; however, in the gullet of the dragon—that is, the maw of the hellish foundation—the properties are manifest in their [wrathful] operation.

13. The hellish foundation and the heavenly one are positioned against each other like day and night. They are near to one another, but neither can grasp the other, nor can one see the other in its true essence. For what is a burning of love in **God** is a burning of wrath in Hell.

14. This hellish abyss also pours itself out to some extent in various places through the elements, especially in the earth as well as in the upper realm. In such places, "fire-pits" <note>original: "Feuer-Pfüle." Likely referring to pockets of spiritual or elemental turbulence.</note> are perceived—sometimes of great cold and sometimes of great heat—especially in great wildernesses and where there are clefts in the earth. Within these dwell the highly damned spirits as well as the highly damned souls of men. Even the evil spirits themselves are terrified by this, for it is the burning hell.

15. Otherwise, their true foundation is not yet entirely manifest, and they must still await a greater judgment. The sun and the water still keep their realm hidden, so that it may not become entirely manifest until the Day of Judgment; it is only manifest within the properties in themselves...

<summary>Böhme describes the nature of the fallen "dragon" as an "outflow of smoke" from the eternal fire, existing in a state of wrath separated from God's light. He explains that hell exists as a dark shadow or "maw" alongside the heavenly realm, though the two remain invisible to one another.</summary>

manifest; which is why the devils also tremble before the Last Judgment.

16. Therefore, understand it correctly: **God** is nothing within the foundation of hell, for within it, He does not flow out according to His love; He is indeed there, but only within Himself; but to the hellish foundation, He is nothing according to love.

17. Thus, hell is also nothing in **God**; it truly exists, but the light does not grasp it; they are toward one another as death and life. Each lives and wills within itself, and both are together like Something and Nothing <note>Böhme uses the terms "Ichts" and "Nichts." "Ichts" is a word he coined to mean "Something" or "Beingness," derived from the word for "I" (Ich), implying a self-aware substance.</note>.

18. **God** is a Something <term>Ichts</term> in the light, and a Nothing in hell; for the eternal unity is a being and power only in the light, and the Lie is a being and power only in its own "un-pleasantness" <note>original: "Annehmlichkeit" (acceptability/pleasantness). In Böhme's usage, this refers to the quality of a spirit's own internal state.</note>.

19. For this reason, the devils stand in great mockery: they are so near to **God** and yet cannot reach Him, and that is also their agony and torment.

->The 14th and 15th Questions.<-

## The 14. Fr. What is the office of the devils in hell? The 15. Fr. Did the foundation of hell take a beginning in time, or has it existed from eternity; or how can it exist eternally or not?

->Summaries.<-

The wrath of **God** has existed from eternity, §. 1. and was first ignited in the fall of Lucifer, 2. which awakened ground cannot be extinguished, 3. because two eternal beginnings must exist within each other. 4.

1. Answer. The foundation has indeed always been from eternity, but not in such a manifestation <note>original: "Offenbarung." In this context, it means the outward expression or making-visible of a hidden quality.</note>; for **God’s** wrath has indeed been from eternity, but not *as* a wrath—not figuratively, or flowed-out—but just as fire lies hidden in wood, or in a stone, until it is awakened.

2. The awakening or ignition—or the gullet of the dragon—took its beginning in the fall of Lucifer as a creature, when the self-will, as the "No," turned away from the "Yes" <note>original: "Jah." Böhme uses "Jah" to represent the divine "Yes" or the affirmative Name of God (Yahweh), which he contrasts with the "Nein" (No) of the self-centered will.</note>.

<meta>catchword: 3. Because</meta>

<summary>This page explains Böhme's dialectical view of God's presence, where God is "Something" in the light but "Nothing" to the darkness of hell. It also begins exploring the origins of hell, suggesting that while God's wrath is an eternal potential, it was only "ignited" into a manifest reality by Lucifer's rebellion.</summary>

<keywords>Ichts, Nichts, God's Wrath, Lucifer, Theosophy, eternity, manifestation, dualism, the Fall, Yes and No</keywords>

--- Page 53 ---
<meta>This page continues the conclusion of the answer to Question 15 regarding the eternal nature of the "ground" of hell and transitions into the final set of questions. It notes that Jacob Böhme passed away before completing the full series of 177 questions, and mentions the English mystic Edward Taylor's role in continuing the work.</meta>

<page-num>43</page-num>
# Fr. 13-18. Contemplation of Divine Revelation.

3. But because such an awakened ground has sprung from the eternal foundation and possesses an eternal will, such a foundation may not pass away; otherwise, the whole of creation would be abolished again, and eternal nature would be extinguished in its own "pleasantness" <note>original: "Annehmlichkeit." In Böhme's mystical vocabulary, this refers to the quality of a spirit's internal state or its "acceptability" to itself.</note>: and if that were to happen, then knowledge and sensation would also be extinguished, as well as the realm of joy.

4. This cannot be, for there must be two eternal beginnings within one another, so that one may be known and felt in the other, and so that the holy angels and souls, along with all heavenly creatures, may praise **God** and rejoice in the good—that they do not dwell in evil, and do not have to be evil. It must, etc. <note>The "etc." indicates a standard theological continuation or a concluding thought left abbreviated in the manuscript.</note>

**Note.** Thus far the dear Theosophist <note>Referring to Jacob Böhme (1575–1624), the author of these writings.</note> has answered these deep questions from his divine gift, after which he was called away by **God** into the rest of the saints. In the meantime, however, the eager seeker will find almost all the remaining questions sufficiently resolved in the other high writings found here, which may be sought in their proper places in the third index. Otherwise, the famous English mystic Edward Taylor, as a great lover of these theosophical writings, has undertaken from the same foundation to answer all the remaining questions according to his gift, and had them printed in England under the title: **Jac. Boehm’s** *Theosophia*, as may be seen in the Biography, Number VI.

**The 16. Question.** Why has **God** poured out such wrath, within which there shall be an eternal corruption?

**The 17. Question.** Since **God** is undivided and remains eternal, what then is His operation in the place of hell; or is there also a certain place of hell, or not?

**The 18. Question.** Where is the place of heaven where the angels dwell, and how is it distinguished from hell? Is it also a certain place; how is this to be understood?

<keywords>Jacob Böhme, Edward Taylor, dualism, wrath of God, heaven and hell, theosophy, eternal nature, sensation</keywords>

<meta>This page transitions from the narrative explanation provided by Böhme’s successors to a list of the specific "Theosophical Questions" that Böhme formulated. While the previous page noted that Böhme did not finish answering all 177 questions, this page records the inquiries themselves, which touch upon cosmology, natural philosophy, and the origin of gender.</meta>

<page-num>44</page-num>
# XVIII. Theosophical Questions. Questions 19–31. <note>original: "XIIX. Quæstiones Theosophicæ." The Roman numeral XIIX is an archaic form of XVIII (18).</note>

The 19th Question. What are the Dominions or Thrones and Princes of the angels, both evil and good, in the invisible world; and how is the spiritual world of eternity to be understood within the visible world? Are they also separated by place and location? Or what is the inner foundation?

The 20th Question. From what was the visible world created, since the Scripture says: **God** has made all things through His Word; how is this to be understood? <note>Referring to the "Logos" or Divine Word from the Gospel of John.</note>

The 21st Question. Since **God** and His Word alone are good, from what then has the evil in the essence of this world flowed? Such as poisonous worms <note>In the 17th century, "worms" often referred to snakes, insects, or any creeping vermin.</note>, animals, herbs, and trees, as well as in the earth and other things?

The 22nd Question. Why must there be strife and opposition in nature?

The 23rd Question. What is the ground of the four elements: how does the separation occur, so that four elements come from One?

The 24th Question. From what were the stars created? and for what use?

The 25th Question. What is the ground of the temporal light of nature <term>Light of Nature: a key concept in Böhme's thought referring to the innate intelligence or guiding principle found within the physical world</term>, and of the darkness? From what does that originate?

The 26th Question. What is the created heaven [formed] from the midst of the waters? And what is the separation of the water above the firmament <note>original: "Veste." This refers to the biblical "expanse" or firmament described in Genesis 1:6-7.</note> from the water under the firmament?

The 27th Question. What is the ground of the male and female kind in the essence of this world? From where has the conjunction <note>original: "Conjunction," meaning union or sexual joining.</note> and desire arisen? Could it not have occurred in one ground, without separation?

The 28th Question. What are the principles <note>original: "Principia."</note> in the spirit of the world, of the upper and lower essence?

The 29th Question. What is the seed <note>original: "Sperma."</note> of the generation of all things?

The 30th Question. What is the difference of the seed between the metals and stones, and the vegetables—such as the herbs, trees, and earthly things?

The 31st Question. How does their conjunction occur, between the female and male kind, from which their seed and growth arises?

<page-num>45</page-num>
<header>Questions 32-46. Contemplation of Divine Revelation.</header>

The 32nd Question. What is the **Tincture** <term>Tincture: In Böhme’s alchemy, this is the hidden spiritual essence or "soul-power" that gives color, life, and medicinal virtue to physical matter.</term> in the **spermatic kind** <note>original: "Spermatischen Art"; referring to the generative seed or reproductive principle in nature.</note>, from which growth and luster <note>original: "Glast"; a shimmer or brilliance reflecting an inner quality.</note> arise?

The 33rd Question. From what have all creatures of mortal life sprouted and been created?

The 34th Question. What was the **Archaeus** <term>Archaeus: A term from Paracelsian medicine referring to the "inner master" or vital force that directs the growth and functions of a living body.</term> and **Separator** <term>Separator: The divine or natural principle that divides the unified "primordial soup" into specific, distinct forms and elements.</term> of their kind and property, which formed them, and still forms them today?

The 35th Question. What are the six days' work of creation, and the Sabbath?

The 36th Question. What is the difference between mortal creatures? And what is their **chaos** <note>In 17th-century theosophy, "chaos" usually refers to the undifferentiated medium or "atmosphere" in which a specific class of beings lives (e.g., water for fish).</note>, in which each species lives, and in which they are distinguished from one another?

The 37th Question. To what end, or why, were mortal creatures created?

The 38th Question. From what was man created with respect to his body?

The 39th Question. What was the "breathing into" <note>Referring to the biblical account in Genesis 2:7 where God breathes life into Adam.</note>, by which man became a living soul?

The 40th Question. What is the immortal life in him, namely the soul; and what is the spirit of man; and what is the outward life of this world within him?

The 41st Question. What is the **Idea** <note>original: "Idea"; Böhme uses this in the Platonic sense of an eternal blueprint or divine prototype.</note> or the image of **God** in man, in which **God** works and dwells?

The 42nd Question. What was the **Paradise** into which **God** created him? Is it changeable and a creaturely thing, or does it stand upon the eternal foundation?

The 43rd Question. Why did **God** initially create only **one** human, and not immediately a man and a woman together, like other species?

The 44th Question. Was the first man created in such a **habit** <term>Habit: From the Latin "habitus," meaning the outward form, condition, or "garment" of a person's being.</term> for eternal life, or for change?

The 45th Question. What kind of image was Adam before his Eve; in what form and shape was he, when he was neither man nor woman, but both? <note>Böhme famously argued that the original human was an androgynous being who contained both masculine and feminine spiritual principles before the physical "fall" into gender.</note>

The 46th Question. Did Adam also have male members before his Eve, and such bones, stomach, intestines, teeth, and all that we have now?

<page-num>46</page-num>
<header>XVIII. Theosophical Questions. Questions 47-57.</header>

The 47th Question. If Adam had been as we are now, how could he have remained in such a state of **impassibility** <term>Impassibility: A state of being incapable of suffering, pain, or injury from external forces.</term> and **indestructibility**?

The 48th Question. What would Adam's eating and drinking have been like in a paradisical way, without worry and need, if he had passed the **test** <note>original: "Proba"; referring to the spiritual trial or probation Adam underwent in the Garden.</note>?

The 49th Question. Would Adam have eaten such fruits in Paradise as the heavenly food will be after this time; or where would that food have gone [within his body], since all beings of this world are earthly and perishable, and he alone was an eternal, heavenly image, and did not need **vanity** <note>original: "Eitelkeit"; in this context, it refers to the fleeting, empty nature of physical matter that eventually decays.</note>?

The 50th Question. Did the **four elements** <note>The classic elements of Earth, Air, Fire, and Water.</note> rule within Adam in his state of innocence, or was there only **one element** <term>One element: Also known as the "Quintessence," Böhme believed this was a pure, unified element that existed before the four gross elements were divided.</term> in the balance of the four elements; and did he feel heat and cold before he fell?

The 51st Question. Could anything have killed him or broken him apart?

The 52nd Question. What would his condition on earth have been like; what would he have done if he had remained in Paradise?

The 53rd Question. What was the earth and its vegetation like before the curse, when it was called a Paradise?

The 54th Question. Could **procreation** have happened without man and woman, since in the resurrection of the dead they shall not be man and woman, but like the angels of God in heaven?

The 55th Question. How could it have been that a man and woman should remain eternal, and would God have wanted to change this human creature, since in the eternal life they shall be like the angels? Was Adam created in that same **angelic form** <term>Angelic form: The "spiritual body" or "heavenly corporeality" that Böhme argues was Adam's original state—androgynous and immortal—before the physical division into male and female.</term> in the beginning, or in another form, since he is to be resurrected and live eternally?

The 56th Question. What were the trees in Paradise, which were lovely to look upon and good to eat?

The 57th Question. What was the **Tree of Life**, and then the **Tree of the Knowledge of Good and Evil**, each in its power, essence, and property?

<summary>This page lists questions regarding Adam’s original immortal nature, focusing on how his body functioned, how he ate, and how he would have reproduced without gender. It contrasts the "heavenly" state of Paradise with the "vanity" and decay of the current material world.</summary>

<header>Questions 58-71. Contemplation of Divine Revelation 47</header>
<meta>catchword: The</meta>

The 58th Question. Why did God create [the tree] <note>Referring to the Tree of Knowledge of Good and Evil.</note>, since He well knew that man would **transgress** <note>original: "vergreiffen"; literally to lay hands upon or seize wrongly.</note> against it?

The 59th Question. Why did God forbid man from it, and what was the underlying cause?

The 60th Question. Why was man intended to rule over all animals on Earth; how could that have been possible, and for what purpose?

The 61st Question. Why did God say: *It is not good that man should be alone*, even though in the beginning He looked upon all His works and said: *They are very good*? And yet of man alone He says: *It is not good that this man should be alone*; why was it not good?

The 62nd Question. Why did God cause a **deep sleep** <note>In Böhme’s theology, this "deep sleep" represents a loss of divine consciousness and the beginning of man's fall into a more material existence.</note> to fall upon Adam as He fashioned a woman for him out of his rib, and what does this signify?

The 63rd Question. How was the woman made out of Adam, and what is signified by the **rib** from his side out of which He made the woman, as Moses writes?

The 64th Question. Did Eve also receive a soul and spirit from Adam’s soul and spirit, or did God give her a new and separate one?

The 65th Question. How did the **separation** <note>original: "Zertrennung"; referring to the division of the original androgynous human into two distinct sexes.</note> of Adam into the woman take place?

The 66th Question. Why did Adam immediately take Eve to himself and say she was his flesh; how was he able to recognize her?

The 67th Question. What was the **serpent** at the Tree of Knowledge of Good and Evil which seduced Eve?

The 68th Question. Why did it persuade Eve, and not Adam, toward the desire and the fruit? What was the fruit, by which they ate their own death?

The 69th Question. What was the **sin** <term>Sin: For Böhme, sin is not just a legal breaking of a rule, but a literal turning of the will away from the divine light and into the "dark world" or self-centeredness.</term>, and how did it become a sin, so that it resulted in enmity toward God?

The 70th Question. Why did God not prevent it, since He had forbidden it, so that it might not have happened?

The 71st Question. How were Adam and Eve’s eyes opened, so that they saw they were **naked**, which they did not recognize before?

<summary>In this series of questions, Böhme probes the mysteries of the Book of Genesis, specifically the creation of Eve from Adam's rib and the psychological and spiritual origins of the first sin. He seeks to understand why God allowed the Fall to happen and what the "deep sleep" of Adam implies about the change in human nature.</summary>
<keywords>Adam, Eve, creation, the Fall, rib, serpent, knowledge, sin, Divine Revelation, deep sleep, nakedness</keywords>

The 72nd Question. What was their shame, that they hid themselves behind the trees of Paradise; and from where did fear and terror come to them?

The 73rd Question. How Adam and Eve, in the Fall, truly died to the Kingdom of Heaven and Paradise, and yet naturally lived to this world? <note>Böhme often explores the idea of "double existence"—how humans can be spiritually dead to the divine realm while remaining physically alive.</note>

The 74th Question. What was the voice of God in the Word when the day grew cool? How did God call Adam back, and how is this to be understood?

The 75th Question. What were the "Seed of the Woman" and the "Serpent-Crusher"? <note>original: "Schlangen-Treter"; a reference to the prophecy in Genesis 3:15 that the offspring of the woman would crush the head of the serpent.</note> What did God speak into them again; was it only an external promise, or an incorporation <note>original: "Einleibung"; suggesting a literal, spiritual merging of divine grace into the human essence.</note> of actual grace?

The 76th Question. What was the curse of the earth, and what occurred through it?

The 77th Question. How were Adam and Eve thrust out of Paradise into the world? And what was the Cherub with the naked sword?

The 78th Question. Why did the first human (born of woman) become a murderer? <note>Referring to Cain, the first child born after the expulsion from Eden.</note>

The 79th Question. What were the offerings of Cain and Abel? Why did they offer them, and what did they accomplish by doing so?

The 80th Question. Why did the murder of Cain happen because of the offering? What was the underlying reason? What kind of archetype <note>original: "Vorbild"; referring to the symbolic or prophetic significance of the two brothers.</note> were these two brothers?

The 81st Question. In what grace was the first world <note>Böhme refers to the "first world" as the era before the Great Flood.</note> saved without the Law, and what was their justification?

The 82nd Question. Was Cain damned because of his sin? What was his doubt regarding grace?

The 83rd Question. Why did God place a mark upon Cain and say: *Whoever kills Cain, his blood shall be avenged sevenfold*?

The 84th Question. Why did Lamech, his descendant, say to his wives Zillah and Adah: *Lamech shall be avenged seventy-seven times*; what does this signify?

The 85th Question. What was the greatest sin of the first world?

The 87th Question. What does the Great Flood signify?

<header>XVIII. Theosophical Questions <note>original: "Quæst. Theosophicæ"</note>. Questions 102 to 113.</header>

Prophecy? By what knowledge and spirit did they <note>Referring to the prophets of the Old Testament.</note> prophesy?

The 102. Question. What was Christ, of whom the prophets prophesied in the Old Testament?

The 103. Question. What was John the Baptist, his forerunner?

The 104. Question. What kind of virgin was Mary (in whom God became man) before she became pregnant?

The 105. Question. Why did she first have to be betrothed to the aged Joseph before she became pregnant by the Holy Spirit; what does this signify?

The 110. Question. Why did Christ walk the earth for thirty years before he took up his office? <note>His "office" refers to his public ministry and preaching.</note> Why did He grow in age and grace with God and men, since He was God Himself and required no growth?

The 111. Question. Why did He allow Himself to be baptized by John with water, since He Himself was the Baptism, who was to baptize with the Holy Spirit?

The 112. Question. Why did Christ have to be tempted in the wilderness for forty days after his baptism? The 113. Question. How was Christ simultaneously in heaven and also on earth?

<page-num>51</page-num>
<header>Questions 114/124. Contemplation of Divine Revelation.</header>
<sig>D 2</sig>

The 114. Question. Why did Christ, while on earth, teach the people about the kingdom of heaven in **parables**?

The 115. Question. Why did Christ not write down His **Gospel** in letters, but only taught it, and afterwards let His **Apostles** write it down?

The 116. Question. Why was it necessary for the **High Priests** and **Scribes**—those who taught the people—to contradict Christ, and to constantly blaspheme Him, and want to kill Him? Why was this not done by the worldly authority or the common crowd; what does that signify? <note>Böhme is exploring why religious institutions often oppose new spiritual revelations more fiercely than civil governments do.</note>

The 117. Question. Why was such a **trial** <note>original: "Proceß"</note> held with mocking, scorning, and scourging before His **Passion**? Why did God allow this to happen?

The 118. Question. Why did the **Teachers of the Law** have to lead Christ to judgment, and yet He had to be put to death by a **pagan authority**? <note>Referring to the Jewish Sanhedrin handing Jesus over to the Roman Governor, Pontius Pilate.</note>

The 119. Question. Why did Christ have to suffer and die? Was God so intent on such a revenge <note>Böhme uses "Rache" (revenge/vengeance) here to probe the traditional doctrine of "divine satisfaction"—the idea that God’s justice required a specific penalty for sin.</note> so that He might be reconciled? Could He not otherwise forgive sin?

The 120. Question. What is the **figure** <note>The term <term>figure</term> refers to "typology," where a physical person or event acts as a symbolic shadow of a deeper spiritual reality.</note> of the two murderers who were hung on the **cross** beside Christ; and why did Christ have to die on a wooden cross, and not in some other way?

The 121. Question. How did Christ, through His dying, strangle death upon the cross; how did that come to pass?

The 122. Question. Why did He have to be nailed to the cross, and why was His side opened with a spear, from which **blood and water** flowed; what does this signify in the **figure**?

The 123. Question. Why did He have to be mocked on the cross?

The 124. Question. Was the **Divine Power** also present in the **blood** that He shed upon the earth?

--- Page 62 ---
<meta>This page continues Jacob Böhme’s series of "Theosophical Questions," moving from the trials of Christ discussed on the previous page to the cosmic and spiritual mysteries of His Crucifixion, Burial, and Resurrection. Böhme seeks to move beyond the literal historical narrative to find the "figure"—the underlying spiritual mechanics—of salvation.</meta>

<page-num>52</page-num>
<header>XVIII. Theosophical Questions. Q. 125–136.</header>
<meta>catchword: The</meta>

The 125. Question. Why did the earth tremble when Christ hung on the **Cross**?

The 126. Question. What is signified by the **darkness** which occurred supernaturally at that time?

The 127. Question. Why did Christ commend His **soul** into His Father’s hands at His death? What were those hands of **God**? <note>In Böhme's thought, the "hands of God" often refer to the different "principles" or powers of the divine nature—the creative power versus the judging power.</note>

The 128. Question. Why did some convert and turn back when they saw what happened during Christ's death, yet the **High Priests** did not? Why did they have to be blind and hardened toward such a work?

The 129. Question. What was Christ’s **descent into hell**, wherein He overcame the **devil** and **death**? <note>This refers to the "Harrowing of Hell," the belief that between His death and resurrection, Christ descended into the realm of the dead to liberate souls.</note>

The 130. Question. How did He preach to the **spirits** who did not believe in the time of **Noah**, as it is written? <note>A reference to 1 Peter 3:19-20, a foundational text for the doctrine of the descent into hell.</note>

The 131. Question. What is signified by His rest in the **grave**, in that He was meant to lie in the grave for 40 hours? <note>Böhme often draws parallels between the "40 hours" in the tomb, the 40 days of the Flood, and the 40 days Christ spent in the wilderness.</note>

The 132. Question. Why did His grave have to be secured with guards; what does it signify that the High Priests wanted to resist God's power and keep Christ in the grave?

The 133. Question. Why does the **Evangelist** say that the angels rolled the great stone away from the door of the grave; could Christ not have risen from the grave otherwise?

The 134. Question. What was the power of His **Resurrection** through death? How did He display <note>original: "schaugetragen"</note> death upon His own body? What did He accomplish by this?

The 135. Question. What kind of gate did He open through death in our **humanity**, into God’s wrath and righteousness, through which we may enter to God? How does this happen? <note>Böhme views Christ's death as a "breaking through" the fiery wrath of God to reach the "light-world" or the heart of God.</note>

The 136. Question. Why did Christ, after His Resurrection, appear first to a woman and not to the disciples?

<summary>This page lists questions regarding the supernatural events surrounding Christ's death and resurrection, focusing on the spiritual significance of the earthquake, the descent into hell, and the opening of a "gate" through God's wrath for humanity.</summary>
<keywords>Crucifixion, Descent into Hell, Resurrection, Divine Wrath, High Priests, Noah, 40 hours, Spiritual Gate</keywords>

--- Page 63 ---
<meta>This page continues the sequence of "Theosophical Questions" by Jacob Böhme. Having previously addressed the Passion and Resurrection of Christ, Böhme now turns to the post-Resurrection appearances, the Ascension, and the mystery of Pentecost. He focuses on the spiritual "figures" or archetypes behind these historical events, particularly how the external history reflects an internal spiritual reality.</meta>

<page-num>53</page-num>
<header>Questions 137-148. Contemplation of Divine Revelation.</header>

The 137. Question. What is signified by the pilgrimage of the two disciples from Jerusalem toward **Emmaus**, when they lamented for their Master in great distress, and **Christ** walked among them, and rebuked and taught them, and yet they did not recognize Him?

The 138. Question. Why did **Christ** eat of the roasted fish with His disciples after His resurrection, and enter in to them through locked doors, and teach them? <note>Böhme is interested in the "resurrection body"—how Christ could be physical enough to eat, yet spiritual enough to pass through walls.</note>

The 139. Question. Why did **Christ** not show Himself to everyone after His resurrection, but only to a few?

The 140. Question. Why did **Christ** walk upon the earth for forty days after His resurrection before He ascended to heaven; what does this signify? <note>In Böhme's system, "40" is a number of testing and completion, mirroring the 40 days of the Flood and the 40 days in the wilderness.</note>

The 141. Question. What is **Christ’s Ascension**, in that He ascended visibly? Where did He go? And where is He now? <note>Böhme often argues that "Heaven" is not a distant place in the stars, but an internal, spiritual state of being present everywhere.</note>

The 142. Question. What is signified by the two men in shining garments who said: "You men, why do you look after Him here? This **Jesus** will come again, just as you have seen Him ascend"?

The 143. Question. Why did the disciples of **Christ** have to wait another nine days for the sending of the **Holy Spirit**, so that it did not happen immediately?

The 144. Question. What is the meaning of this: that the disciples of **Christ** had to wait and remain together until the **Holy Spirit** came?

The 145. Question. What is the feast of **Pentecost**; how did the outpouring of the **Holy Spirit** occur; and how was the <term>bond of tongues</term> dissolved in them? <note>The "bond of tongues" refers to the confusion of languages at the Tower of Babel; Pentecost is seen as the reversal of that curse, where humanity is reunited in one spiritual understanding.</note>

The 146. Question. How is the distinction of languages among them to be understood, in that they all spoke all languages at once in <note>original: "Einem Sensu"</note> one sense <note>meaning: in one single meaning or understanding</note>, so that all nations understood them?

The 147. Question. How does this outpouring of His Spirit—proceeding from **Christ’s** death, resurrection, and ascension—benefit us; and how may this also occur within us?

The 148. Question. What are the **Literal Word** and the **Living Word**, Christ, together in such an outpouring; how are they distinguished? Since not everyone [receives] the H[oly]...

<sig>D 3</sig>
<meta>catchword: Spirit</meta>

<summary>This page lists questions regarding the spiritual significance of Christ's post-Resurrection life, His Ascension, and the miracle of Pentecost. Böhme seeks to explain the "bond of tongues" and the distinction between the literal word of scripture and the living word of the Spirit.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Jacob Böhme’s "Theosophical Questions." The previous page ended mid-sentence during a discussion on the events of Pentecost and the nature of spiritual hearing. <page-num>54</page-num>
<header>XVIII. Theosophical Questions. Fr. 149-155.</header>

...heard the Spirit teaching from the mouths of the Apostles; for some said: "They are full of sweet wine"; did these people hear merely human words, but did not hear **Christ** teaching in His resurrection? <note>This fragment refers to the Day of Pentecost in Acts 2, where onlookers mocked the Apostles, mistaking their spiritual ecstasy for drunkenness. Böhme asks if those who lack the Spirit only hear the external "human" sounds rather than the living Christ.</note>

The 149. Question. How does **Christ** Himself teach presently in the **office of preaching**, while yet sitting at the right hand of the power of **God**, or in whom does He teach? What is a **shepherd** <note>German: *Hirte*, referring here to a pastor or spiritual leader.</note> in the Spirit of Christ, and what is a teacher of the **letter** without the Spirit of Christ, each in his own office?

The 150. Question. What is the **Office of the Keys** <term>Office of the Keys: The authority given to the church to forgive or retain sins, based on John 20:23.</term>? How may it be used rightly, or who is worthy of such an office of the keys and capable of it? How is this to be understood? Does **Christ** Himself maintain the office, and is He Himself the office, or has He given it freely to men so that they might forgive sins without Christ’s Spirit, or how does that happen?

The 151. Question. What is His **Testament** in the **Last Supper** with bread and wine; how is **Christ** truly partaken of <note>original: *genossen*, meaning to enjoy, consume, or participate in the sacrament.</note>; what kind of flesh and blood is it, and what is the "mouth" intended for it?

The 152. Question. What and where is the **place within man** where **Christ’s** flesh and blood remains, since He said: "Whoever eats my flesh and drinks my blood remains in me, and I in him, and whoever does not eat this has no life in me"?

The 153. Question. How is and how does man become a **branch on the vine of Christ**, and how does **Christ** dwell in him while yet sitting at the right hand of **God** in heaven? How can He also sit at the right hand of **God** within man, while the **outer man** is not the same thing? <note>Böhme frequently explores the paradox of Christ being "locally" in heaven while being "spiritually" present within the soul of the believer.</note>

The 154. Question. What kind of a Christian is a **nominal Christian** <term>Titel-Christ: Literally a "Title-Christian"; someone who claims the name of Christ outwardly but has not experienced inner spiritual transformation.</term> who exists outside of **Christ**, who only comforts himself and credits **Christ’s** merit to himself, but is un-reborn by **Christ’s** Spirit and lives like an animal? Does he also belong to **Christ** in such a state <note>original: *Wirckung*, referring to the "operation" or "working" of the Spirit.</note>, or what does he receive in the **Supper of Christ**?

The 155. Question. Can **Christ’s** flesh and blood also be partaken of by believers **outside of the ordained Testament** and its formal usage? Or how might that happen?

<summary>In this series of questions, Böhme explores the mystical nature of the sacraments, the authority of the clergy, and the difference between a purely intellectual faith and a true, internal union with Christ.</summary>
<keywords>Office of the Keys, Last Supper, Eucharist, Nominal Christian, New Birth, Mystical Union, Sacraments, Inner Man</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Jacob Böhme’s "Theosophical Questions," specifically the 177 questions concerning divine revelation. The previous page (54) concluded with Question 155, which began an inquiry into the nature of the Last Supper and the physical elements of the sacrament. <page-num>55</page-num>
<header>Questions 156–167. Consideration of Divine Revelation.</header>

The 156. Question. Why has **Christ** ordained and instituted such a **Testament** <note>original: *Testament*, used here interchangeably with the Sacrament of the Lord’s Supper</note>, and said: That as often as we do it, we should do it in his **remembrance**? For what benefit does it happen with bread and wine? and not without the same; or can it also be enjoyed without bread and wine?

The 157. Question. Is the true sacramental participation bound solely to the first **Apostolic practice**, or do men also have the power to change such an order, as has happened?

The 158. Question. Is the **Testament** effective in the altered order as well, or not?

The 159. Question. What are the **scholars** <note>German: *Gelehrten*, referring to the university-trained theologians of the day who were often at odds with Böhme’s mystical views.</note> doing when they slander and revile one another for the sake of **Christ’s Testament** and the **New Covenant**, and for that reason give one another over to the **Devil**? Do they also perform Christ’s office? Is it right or wrong; do they do such things as servants of Christ; or whom do they serve thereby?

The 160. Question. What is the mark of a **true Christian** on earth, by which one can distinguish them from the **nominal Christians** <note>original: *Titel-Christen*, literally "title-Christians" or those who are Christian in name only.</note>?

The 161. Question. What is a **Christian** properly, from within and without? How is he a **temple of the Holy Spirit**, in that the **Kingdom of God** is manifest within? How does he walk in **Heaven** and on **Earth** at the same time?

The 162. Question. What is the **Antichrist** on earth within Christendom?

The 163. Question. What is **Babel**, the **Beast**, and the **Whore** in the **Revelation** <note>original: *Apocalypsi*, the Book of Revelation</note>? <term>Babel: For Böhme, Babel represents the "city of confusion," or the divided, institutionalized churches that fight over doctrine rather than living in the Spirit.</term>

The 164. Question. What is its downfall, and how does it happen that the same **seven-headed beast** is cast into the abyss?

The 165. Question. How does **Christ** then take possession of the kingdom within the person, when this beast is slain?

The 166. Question. What is the true **New Rebirth** <term>New Rebirth: German *Wiedergeburt*. In Böhme's theology, this is the essential internal transformation where the soul is born again into the light of God.</term> in the Spirit of Christ? Does it happen in this time, or after this time?

The 167. Question. What is the **death** of a true Christian; what dies in him?

<meta>catchword: Die</meta>

<summary>Böhme poses a series of questions exploring the necessity of the physical elements in the Eucharist, the internal spiritual markers of a true Christian, and the identity of the "Antichrist" and "Babel" within the corrupted church systems of his time.</summary>

<page-num>56</page-num>
<header>18. Theosophical Questions. <note>original: *Quæstiones Theosophicæ*. In Böhme's time, "Theosophy" referred to a specific type of mystical insight into the nature of God and the cosmos.</note> Questions 168–177.</header>

The 168. Question. What is the death of the **ungodly**, such that it is called an **eternal death**?

The 169. Question. Where does the **soul** go when it departs from the body, whether it be **blessed** or not?

The 170. Question. What is its **activity** and **life** until the **Last Day**? <note>German: *jüngsten Tag*, literally "the youngest day," a common term for the Day of Judgment.</note>

The 171. Question. What is the **Last Judgment**? Or how does it come to pass?

The 172. Question. How does the **resurrection of the dead** occur, and what is it that rises?

The 173. Question. How does this **world pass away**, and what remains of it?

The 174. Question. What will happen after this world, when **God** shall be **all in all**, and when all **dominions** <note>original: *Herrschaften*, referring to earthly hierarchies, powers, or lordships that Böhme believes will be superseded by divine presence.</note> are abolished?

The 175. Question. Where will **Hell**, and then the **eternal dwelling** of the **Saints**, be located?

The 176. Question. What will be the **conduct and state** <note>original: *Thun und Lassen*, an idiom meaning "doing and leaving," referring to one's entire manner of life and action.</note> of the Saints and the **Damned**?

The 177. Question. What will be the **eternal joy** of the Saints, and the **eternal torment** of the ungodly; or might a **change** <note>Böhme here hints at the controversial question of whether the state of the damned is truly immutable or if a restoration (apocatastasis) is possible.</note> yet occur?

## TABLES OF PRINCIPLES,
<note>original: "TABULÆ PRINCIPIORUM"</note>
or
# Tables of the Three Principles of Divine Revelation.*
### Explanation regarding the Schema, and regarding the three tables of Divine Revelation.

In these (following) three tables, it is explained and indicated how the hidden GOD revealed Himself out of Himself through the breathing forth of His power: What heaven and hell, the world, angels, devils, and all creatures, together with all beings and their inner workings <note>The original "Wesens und Webens" implies both the essence of a thing and its active, "weaving" movement.</note> are: From whence evil and good, light and darkness, life and death, friend and foe, hardness and softness originate; And how the transformation of all beings occurred; How the good is transformed into an evil, and the evil into a good: Also presented therein is how all things are good and useful in the <term>Ground</term> <note>The "Ground" or "Unground" is Böhme’s term for the absolute, undifferentiated origin of all existence.</note> from which they initially sprouted; And how all motions exist in necessity.

2. And specifically, the three <term>Principles</term> <note>Böhme uses the word "Principles" to describe the three distinct realms of reality: the dark fire-world, the light spiritual-world, and the physical world we inhabit.</note> of Divine Revelation are indicated herewith, showing how they originate from a single ground, according to eternity and time.

3. Namely, the first Principle with the eternal darkness, as a receptivity of properties, from which sensitivity, willing, and life originate, which reaches with its ground into the fire.

4. The second Principle is understood in the light with the Angelic or Power-world, wherein the outflowing—

# TABLES OF THE PRINCIPLES,
->or<-
## Tables of the three Principles of Divine Revelation. *
### Explanation of the Diagram, and of the three Tables of Divine Revelation.

In these (following) three tables, it is explained and indicated how the hidden GOD revealed Himself out of Himself, through His breathing out of power: What Heaven and Hell, World, Angel, Devil, and all creatures, along with all beings and essences are: From what Evil and Good, Light and Darkness, Life and Death, Friend and Foe, Hardness and Softness originate; and how the transformation of all beings occurs; how Good transforms into an Evil, and Evil into a Good: It is also presented therein how all things, in the ground from which they initially sprouted, are good and useful; and how all movements exist in inevitability.

2. And specifically, the three <term>Principles</term> <note>The "Principles" (Latin: *Principia*) are the three fundamental aspects of Böhme's cosmos: the dark fire-world, the light light-world, and the outward material world.</note> of Divine Revelation are hereby indicated, as to how they originate from a single ground according to Eternity and Time.

3. As the first Principle with the eternal darkness, as a <term>receptivity</term> <note>original: "Annehmlichkeit." In Böhme’s mystical physics, this refers to the "taking in" or contraction of qualities that creates sensitivity and the foundation of existence.</note> of the properties, from which sensitivity, willing, and life originate, which with its ground reaches into the fire.

4. The second Principle is understood in the Light with the Angelic or Power-world, in which the outflow

<meta>This page continues the numbered explanation of the "Tables of the Principles," completing the definition of the Second Principle and detailing the specific contents of the three tables within the diagram (Schema).</meta>

58
<header>XIX. Tables of the Principles.</header>

...of Divine power and will, revealed through the magical fire in the Light with fire-flaming love, wherein one understands the Kingdom of God.

5. The third <term>Principle</term> <note>The Third Principle represents the physical, material universe we inhabit, which Böhme sees as a temporary "outflow" of the eternal spiritual worlds.</note> is the visible elemental world with its hosts, which is an outflow from the first and second Principles, through the movement and outbreathing of Divine power and will; within it, the spiritual world is mirrored according to both Light and Darkness, and has entered into a created form.

6. In the <term>Diagram</term> <note>original: "Schemate"</note> (**ADONAI**) <note>Hebrew: "Lord," a name for God used here to signify the Divine essence beyond all manifestation.</note> it is indicated what God is in Himself, outside of nature and creation. In the **first table**, the outbreathing of the Divine Word through **Wisdom** <note>Böhme refers to *Sophia*, the mirror of Divine reflection.</note> is shown: how the breathing of Divine power creates a <term>counter-response</term> <note>original: "Gegenwurf." A central concept in Böhme’s thought, meaning the "mirroring" or "objectification" of the Divine will that allows it to perceive itself.</note> for itself, and how the outflowing will introduces itself into a state of receptivity—specifically into **seven properties**—leading toward the eternal nature of sensitivity and reality. In this reality, the eternal will of God is recognized naturally as a Creator of being, wherein primarily the angelic and soulful ground is understood through eternal, spiritual fire and Light.

7. In the **second table**, the visible world is understood as an outflow of that same inner spiritual power-world; it shows how the properties of the inner ground have divided themselves and were again brought into a counter-response, from which the stars, elements, and creatures took and received their origin.

8. In the **third table**, **Man** is understood according to all three Principles, as a true counterpart-image of God from eternity and time: what he is in soul, spirit, and body; likewise, what he was in **Paradise** in the first creation, and what he became in the **Fall** through the spirit of error; what the venom of the serpent is within him, and how **Christ** came to help him again and to give him new birth, and what he is in Christ in the new **Rebirth**.

<meta>catchword: DIAGRAM.</meta>

<summary>This page defines the Third Principle as the physical world and explains the three "Tables" of Böhme's philosophy: the spiritual foundation of nature, the origin of the physical cosmos, and the spiritual anatomy and redemption of Man.</summary>
<keywords>Third Principle, Adonai, Wisdom, Gegenwurf, Seven Properties, Stars and Elements, Fall of Man, Rebirth in Christ</keywords>

## SCHEMA.

->Wherein God is considered according to His Essence in Unity, what He is as the Trinity outside of Nature and Creature; so that He fulfills all things, and yet requires no place.<-
